CLEAPSS HazCards - Compound Reference

Condensed reference from the supplied CLEAPSS HazCards PDF. This version focuses on substances, formulas, warnings, incompatibilities and waste. Consult the original Hazcard/GL 120 for complete activity-specific guidance.

1AAluminium
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 7

Compounds / substances

Aluminium powder

Al MW 26.98
Direct compound anchor: #compound-1a-1-aluminium-powder

Aluminium foil

Al MW 26.98
Direct compound anchor: #compound-1a-2-aluminium-foil

Warnings

1A Risk Assessment Guidance Aluminium Aluminium powder Al (26.98) Flammable solid [H228].
In contact with water releases flammable gases [H261].
Currently not classified as hazardous.

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • WATER. Under very hot conditions, hydrogen is formed. ACIDS and ALKALIS. Hydrogen is formed.
  • ALCOHOLS. Violent reactions occur after a lengthy induction period.
  • HALOGENS, ORGANOHALOGENS. SULFUR. Violent or explosive reactions occur.
  • METAL OXIDES, NITRATES(V), CHLORATES and other SOLID OXIDISING AGENTS. Explosive mixtures form. DANGER
  • MERCURY and its COMPOUNDS (includes solutions). Rapid oxidation of aluminium occurs, may cause ignition.

Important notes

  • Thermite reaction (TT): See Thermite reaction. Aluminium TT
  • Powdered aluminium is particularly dangerous if blown into a powder (Y7) Bunsen burner flame. Do not use aluminium powder in reactions DANGER with acids, alkalis, chlorine or bromine; use small pieces of foil. (See reverse)

Waste / disposal

  • : W2, WSpec; see below
  • Eye protection and/or other control measures may be advised for some procedures. Check activity-specific guidance and/or GL 120. Currently not classified as Aluminium hazardous but see notes Y7 foil on reverse. Disposal: W3, W8 Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE for small amounts of aluminium powder:
  • WSpec: Work in a fume cupboard. Ensure no naked flames. Add 10 g of the powder to 1 litre of 2 M Disposal hydrochloric acid (in a bucket or large beaker). Ensure solution remains acidic (use an indicator); add more acid if necessary. Leave mixture in the fume cupboard for an hour or until reaction appears to be complete. Pour resulting solution down a foul-water drain, diluting it further with a bucket of water.

1BGallium
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 9

Compounds / substances

Gallium

Ga MW 69.72
Direct compound anchor: #compound-1b-1-gallium

Warnings

Gallium Ga (69.72) Causes severe skin burns and eye damage [H314].
May be corrosive to metals [H290].

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • METALS (ESPECIALLY ALUMINIUM/ALUMINIUM ALLOYS). Gallium attacks metals by forming amalgams.
  • HALOGENS. Violent, exothermic reactions occur. DANGER
  • HYDROGEN PEROXIDE, HYDROCHLORIC ACID. Risk of explosions.

Important notes

  • Gallium TT
  • Melting gallium (TT): Place the container inside a plastic bag (to DANGER protect the label). Stand this in a container of warm water.
  • Demonstrations using gallium (TT): See Supplementary Risk CORROSIVE Assessment Investigating the properties of gallium metal (SRA 037) (skin, eyes, metal) for detailed guidance; this includes Making a gallium spoon.

Waste / disposal

  • : W3, W1 Disposal Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120).

Waste / disposal

  • : W7 or W4; see notes below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE for small amounts only:
  • W7 (hydrated salt solutions): Dilute solutions to below 0.4 M. Disposal
  • W4 (anhydrous solids): Use a fume cupboard. Add no more than 60 g of anhydrous solid in small portions to 1 litre of 1 M sodium carbonate solution. Allow reaction to subside before adding further portions. Pour the resulting slurry down a foul-‐water drain with further dilution. chloride Phenylamine: Very toxic to aquatic life [H400] (with long lasting effects [H410], diphenylamine).

  • Phenylammonium alternative. See Safer chemicals, safer reactions. chloride
  • Diazotisation/azo dye preparation: The solid phenylammonium DANGER chloride is easier to transfer than phenylamine and avoids the use (See reverse) of a fume cupboard. Sulfanilic acid and ethyl 4-aminobenzoate are safer alternatives. See Safer chemicals, safer reactions.

Waste / disposal

  • : W5, see below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE for small amounts only: Disposal
  • W5: Add no more than 1 cm3 (or 1 g) of the amine to 100 cm3 of 1 M ethanoic acid. Flush the resulting mixture down a foul-water drain with further dilution.

7Ammonium dichromate(VI), (NH4)2Cr2O7
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 33

Compounds / substances

No formula-bearing compound entry was detected on the first page.

Warnings

7 Risk Assessment Guidance Ammonium dichromate(VI), (NH4)2Cr2O7 Ammonium dichromate(VI) (NH4)2Cr2O7 (252.07) May intensify fire; oxidiser [H272].
Toxic if swallowed [H301].
Harmful in contact with skin [H312].
Causes severe skin burns and eye damage [H314].
May cause an allergic skin reaction [H317].
Fatal if inhaled [H330].
May cause allergy or asthma symptoms or breathing difficulties if inhaled [H334].
May cause genetic defects [H340].
May cause cancer [H350].
Causes damage to organs through prolonged or repeated exposure [H372].
Very toxic to aquatic life with long lasting effects [H410].
Currently not classified as hazardous.

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • ALUMINIUM, MAGNESIUM, CARBON, SULFUR, PHOSPHORUS, COMBUSTIBLE MATERIALS, including ORGANIC SUBSTANCES.
  • The reactions are violent and may become explosive.
  • Never mix ammonium dichromate with metals, especially if powdered (eg, aluminium, magnesium).
  • DANGER Never heat ammonium dichromate in a confined space; explosion risk.
  • See Other notes (reverse).

Important notes

  • Solutions Irritant (skin, eyes), ≥ 0.04 M
  • Weighing the solid: Weigh on a balance placed in a fume < 0.1 M and ≥ 0.004 M Sensitiser (skin, respiratory) cupboard that is not switched on. Have sash partially down. Serious health hazard (RE), ≥ 0.04 M Wipe down work area with a damp paper towel. Serious health hazard (CMR)

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • CARBON, SULFUR, POWDERED METALS. Readily ignites - is extremely sensitive to friction or impact. Barium peroxide is dangerous in contact with:
  • COMBUSTIBLE MATERIAL including ORGANIC SUBSTANCES. Violent or explosive reactions occur. May ignite spontaneously, particularly if damp.
  • POWDERED METALS. Dangerous reactions occur.

  • WATER. Vigorous reaction occurs forming a corrosive solution. Oxygen gas is rapidly evolved. DANGER

  • peroxide
  • Thermite reaction (TT): Some published methods may suggest the use of (See reverse) barium nitrate(V) or barium peroxide in an initiator mix. This guidance also takes into account the requirements of the current Explosives Regulations (see Fireworks and explosives).
  • Precipitation reactions: Use sodium carbonate instead of potassium Barium nitrate(V) Currently not classified chromate(VI) for investigations involving the precipitation of barium as hazardous Y7 compounds. solutions

  • WSpec for barium peroxide: Add no more than 5 g in small portions to 1 litre of 1 M sulfuric(VI) acid in a beaker. Leave stirring overnight. Allow solid to settle then decant the liquid layer down a foul-water drain with plenty of water. The solid barium sulfate(VI) can go in the normal refuse, W8.

May cause respiratory irritation [H335].
May cause drowsiness or dizziness [H336].

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • AIR. HEAT. Flammable in air if heated above room temperature. Emits toxic fumes (hydrogen chloride, carbon monoxide, phosgene) if heated to decomposition or if involved in combustion.
  • METHANOL. In the presence of methanol, the mixture is flammable at room temperature.
  • REACTIVE METALS (eg, ALKALI METALS, MAGNESIUM, ALUMINIUM). STRONG BASES. STRONG OXIDISING AGENTS. Violent or explosive reactions can occur.
  • CONCENTRATED NITRIC(V) ACID. It may dissolve explosively and the solution can be detonated. WARNING

Important notes

  • TT
  • Using dichloromethane: Some commercial uses (eg, paint strippers) are (Y12) now subject to EU-wide restrictions but dichloromethane is still found in a wide range of applications (eg, solvents, cleaning agents, adhesives). Avoid the use of dichloromethane
  • Science activities: Alternatives will be available for most school-based in school practical activities. science practical activities.
  • Design & Technology activities: See CLEAPSS Design & Technology Check activity-specific guidance for alternatives. Model Risk Assessments.

40AEthanol (and IDA)
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 143

Compounds / substances

Ethanol Ethyl alcohol

CH3CH2OH MW 46.07
Direct compound anchor: #compound-40a-1-ethanol-ethyl-alcohol

Warnings

Ethanol Ethyl alcohol CH3CH2OH (46.07) Pure ethanol: Highly flammable liquid and vapour [H225].
IDA is also: Harmful if swallowed [H302].
May cause damage to organs [H371].
Some suppliers may also include: Causes serious eye irritation [H319] for ethanol or IDA.

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • BROMINE. Violent reaction may occur.
  • MERCURY(II) and SILVER NITRATE(V). Explosive fulminates may be formed.
  • OXIDISING AGENTS. Uncontrollable reactions take place, especially with NITRIC(V) ACID.
  • PLATINUM. Finely divided metal (eg, platinised electrodes, catalysts) may cause ethanol to burst into flame.
  • POTASSIUM. An explosive reaction will occur. DANGER

Important notes

  • Heating: Do not use a naked flame to heat a highly flammable liquid. Use hot water from a kettle or use electrically-heated baths. Boiling point of ethanol: 78.3 oC. If ethanol vapour in a container catches fire, cover the container with a heatproof mat or a damp cloth. DANGER Y7 IDA
  • Use as a fuel: Do not allow students to fill/refill crucibles or spirit burners, Highly flammable. TT only. The use of a spirit burner should be dedicated to a specific Harmful (ingestion). alcohol. Ensure the wick fits tightly in its holder and the holder fits tightly May cause damage to organs. in the container. See Safer chemicals, safer reactions and spirit burners. (See also note on reverse)
  • Evaporation/cooling experiments: Alcohol-based hand gels may be used to experience the cooling effect of evaporation. Check the health warnings provided with such products BEFORE use. Apply only a small Aqueous quantity to the back of a hand. Do not use surgical spirits. solutions of See Recipe 2
  • Do not use methanol in place of ethanol (or IDA) where the latter is ethanol/IDA specified for chemical reactions.

48Hydrogen, H2(g)
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 179

Compounds / substances

Hydrogen

H2 MW 2.02
Direct compound anchor: #compound-48-1-hydrogen

Warnings

48 Risk Assessment Guidance Hydrogen, H2(g) Hydrogen H2 (2.02) Extremely flammable gas [H220].
If cylinder/canister: Contains gas under pressure; may explode if heated [H280].

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • OXYGEN, CHLORINE. Explosive reactions occur.
  • TRANSITION METALS and TRANSITION METAL OXIDES. These substances, under certain circumstances, lower the activation energy and ignition temperature of the reaction between hydrogen and oxygen.
  • Hydrogen/air mixtures are explosive over the range of 4% - 75% by volume of hydrogen. The ignition temperature of these mixtures is 500 oC, which is lower than the temperature of a Bunsen burner flame. Ignition can occur well below this temperature in the presence of transition metals or their oxides. DANGER
  • Never oil or grease gas cylinder valves or fittings.
  • Never attempt to breathe hydrogen to change the pitch of the voice.

Important notes

  • Hydrogen gas TT Extremely flammable
  • Preparation of hydrogen gas (large-scale; TT): Recipe 44. Wear splash-proof goggles. (Y7) Protect the face (eg, face-shield). If dry gas is required, use granules of anhydrous If gas cylinder: calcium chloride as the drying agent. Do NOT use concentrated sulfuric acid.
  • Hydrogen gas explosions (demonstrations; TT): à Risk of shattering apparatus; risk of fire: H2 / air and H2 / O2 mixtures. à Risk of damage to hearing: Especially, but not only, H2 / O2 mixtures.
  • Wear splash-proof goggles/face-shield (demonstrator). Use safety screens.
  • Wear ear defenders (demonstrator). Open some windows. Chemically-generated hydrogen gas
  • Warn others in the vicinity about loud noises. Failure to flush all air from gas generating
  • Students: At least 2 m away. Wear eye protection. Place hands over ears. apparatus results in impure hydrogen being
  • Do not ignite gas near combustible materials (eg, ceiling tiles, wall posters). collected which, if ignited, could explode. It is à For a range of effective and safe demonstration activities, see Safer chemicals, difficult to fill balloons using a gas generator. safer reactions (Guide L195) and the Supplementary RA section of the website. Do For these reasons, the use of hydrogen gas NOT exceed the recommended quantities of reagents/gas volumes. cylinders/canisters will be advised for some

Waste / disposal

  • : W6 (gas); see also note below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE:
  • Canisters (non-refillable): it may be possible to dispose of empty canisters into the normal waste or at some local Disposal waste recycling centres. The used canister needs to be punctured to make it safe for disposal; a tool to do this safely is available from suppliers. Check supplier’s information.

Note: 0.01 - 0.1 M solutions are suitable for many activities (eg, testing for Aqueous Currently not classified starch, work with enzymes, titrations with sodium thiosulfate). solutions* as hazardous but see Y7
  • Weighing the solid: weigh in a small beaker or weighing boat, not on <1M Other notes. paper. Iodine rapidly corrodes balance pans.

58ALithium

Lithium

Direct compound anchor: #compound-58a-1-lithium

Warnings

58A Risk Assessment Guidance Lithium Lithium Li (6.9) In contact with water releases flammable gases which may ignite spontaneously [H260].
Causes severe skin burns and eye damage [H314].

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • NITRIC(V) ACID. The metal ignites on contact. DANGER
  • 1, 1, 1-TRICHLOROETHANE and similar compounds. The metal reacts explosively.
  • MERCURY. A violent reaction can occur.

Important notes

  • Preparing lithium for use (TT): All equipment must be dry. Use forceps to place a piece of lithium onto a tile. Use a sharp knife to cut off required TT piece. Immediately return the unwanted lithium to its bottle and close lid. Lithium Dab the cut piece of lithium with a paper tissue/filter paper to remove the (Y9) DANGER oil (medicinal paraffin). Do not use organic solvents (eg, petroleum spirit (See reverse) or hexane) to remove the oil.
  • Heating lithium metal (TT): Wear splash-proof goggles. Protect the face. Use safety screens. Lithium has been known to explode when heated on porcelain, so heat on the ceramic centre of a gauze, or on a combustion spoon.

Waste / disposal

  • : W5, see note below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE for small amounts only: Disposal
  • W5: Add only a few small pieces of lithium at a time to 1 M ethanoic acid and allow reaction to subside before adding further pieces. The reaction is not usually violent.

59A Risk Assessment Guidance Magnesium Magnesium turnings, powder and ribbon Mg (24.30) Magnesium turnings/powder: Flammable solid [H228].
Self-heating in large quantities; may catch fire [H252].
In contact with water releases flammable gases [H261].

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • 1, 1, 1-TRICHLOROETHANE and similar compounds. The metal reacts violently.
  • METHANOL. The metal reacts violently after a lengthy induction period.
  • BROMINE and CHLORINE. The metal reacts violently.
  • SULFUR. The metal reacts violently and explosively. DANGER
  • SILVER NITRATE(V). When added to the metal, an explosion may occur in the presence of water.
  • OXIDISING AGENTS. Explosive mixtures are formed with the metal.
  • SULFATES(VI) and METAL OXIDES. Mixtures of solid with the metal (especially the powder) may be explosive. Take steps to prevent theft. Reels of magnesium ribbon should not be left out in the laboratory. It is good practice to have a limited number of pre-cut lengths and to hand these out to students as needed.

Important notes

  • Powdered magnesium is particularly dangerous if blown into a Bunsen flame. DANGER
  • Viewing burning magnesium: An intense white light is emitted (mainly visible but Magnesium TT (See reverse) some in UV-A region) that can cause unpleasant dazzle and after-images that may ribbon (Y7) persist for some time. The recommended method for viewing the burning metal is to wear eye protection and view through a passive welding filter lens, shade 9. Note: viewing through fingers, sunglasses, smoked glass blue glass or polaroid filters is no longer recommended. For data about the light emitted from burning Take steps to prevent theft. magnesium & guidance on welding filter lenses, see Ultraviolet (UV) light sources. Reels of magnesium ribbon should not NEVER look directly at magnesium...
  • Reaction of magnesium powder with copper(II) oxide (TT): Use a maximum of number of pre-cut lengths and to hand 0.25 g of each reagent. Do not exceed these amounts unless the employer holds these out to students as needed. an explosives certificate issued by the police and the method has been discussed with CLEAPSS. See also Fireworks and explosives.

Waste / disposal

  • : W5; see below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE for small amounts only: Disposal
  • W5: Reaction of acid with turnings/powder generates a lot of heat. Add in small portions to 1 M ethanoic acid.

69 Risk Assessment Guidance Oxygen, O2(g) Oxygen O2 (31.99) May cause or intensify fire; oxidiser [H270].
If gas cylinder, will also be: Contains gas under pressure; may explode if heated [H280].

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • COMBUSTIBLE MATERIALS. These will burn more fiercely in an atmosphere of oxygen.
  • CONCENTRATED AMMONIA SOLUTION. Explosions can occur.
  • Combustible materials burn much more fiercely in even slightly oxygen-enriched air. This is unlikely to be a risk in schools, even if a cylinder leak occurs, but all science staff should be aware of the possibility.
  • Never oil/grease gas cylinder valves/fittings. Under pressure, hydrocarbon/oxygen mixtures can cause explosions. DANGER
  • Liquid oxygen is particularly dangerous to work with and is NOT recommended for use in schools.

Important notes

  • (Y7) If gas cylinder:
  • Preparation of oxygen gas (large-scale; TT): See Recipe 64
  • Making/testing of oxygen gas (small-scale; student): Wear eye protection. WARNING Various methods are possible using hydrogen peroxide solutions (≤ 20 ‘vol’) (See reverse) with manganese(IV) oxide, potassium iodide or yeast as the catalyst.
  • Problems sometimes occur (i.e. little or no oxygen is evolved) if the dilute hydrogen peroxide solution has already decomposed (see Recipe 45). Oxygen gas canisters (non-refillable)
  • Some supplies of manganese(IV) oxide have failed to catalyse the decomposition of hydrogen peroxide into oxygen gas and water. If this There have been problems with the happens, contact your supplier and ask for a replacement. manufacture of canisters/canister valves due
  • Do NOT use so-called ‘oxygen mixture’ (potassium chlorate(V) & to the oxidising properties of oxygen. manganese(IV) oxide) to prepare oxygen gas on any scale. Refillable cylinders (various sizes) are
  • Hydrogen/oxygen gas explosions (demonstrations; TT): See Hazcard 48. available on a rental basis.

73APhosphorus, P (white, yellow)
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 247

Compounds / substances

No formula-bearing compound entry was detected on the first page.

Warnings

White phosphorus yellow phosphorus P (30.97) Catches fire spontaneously if exposed to air [H250].
Fatal if swallowed [H300].
Causes severe skin burns and eye damage [H314].
Fatal if inhaled [H330].
Very toxic to aquatic life [H400].

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • OXIDISING AGENTS, particularly CHLORATES and METAL OXIDES. Explosive mixtures form.
  • AIR, HEAT. Ignites in air at 34 oC. Emits toxic fumes. DANGER

Important notes

  • Refreshing the storage water in containers of white phosphorus: Do not Phosphorus empty out the existing water and then refill. Instead, gently flush through TT with tap water (cold). Ensure that the water level remains above the solid so (white/yellow) that the phosphorus pieces do not become exposed to the air. DANGER
  • Cutting white phosphorus: Do not do this on your own, work with an (See reverse) experienced colleague. Seek detailed guidance/training before attempting this procedure for the first time (contact CLEAPSS if necessary). White phosphorus is a hard solid and is very difficult to cut. The cutting process is always carried out with the solid immersed in water (to prevent ignition) in a deep plastic bowl (never use a glass bowl).

Waste / disposal

  • : W1 Disposal Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120).

73B
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 249

Compounds / substances

Warnings

Red phosphorus P (30.97) Flammable solid [H228].
Harmful to aquatic life with long lasting effects [H412].

Waste / disposal

  • : W1, W6; see notes below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE:
  • W1 (decomposed/unwanted stock): If there is evidence that the solid has reacted with water vapour (see front of Hazcard), dispose of the container and contents via a registered waste contractor.
  • W6 (small, ~ 1 g or less, quantities): Use a fume cupboard. Place the sample of red phosphorus in a Disposal small metal crucible firmly supported on a tripod (see also Make-‐it guide -‐ bottle top crucibles). Direct a small Bunsen (or kitchen blowtorch) flame onto the top of the solid sample to ignite the phosphorus. The solid should continue to burn with no further heating. Once cool, place crucible and contents into a 250 cm3 beaker of water and leave overnight. Flush the resulting dilute phosphoric acid solution (~ 0.1 M) down a foul-‐water drain with further dilution. In contact with water releases flammable gases which may ignite spontaneously [H260].
Causes severe skin burns and eye damage [H314].

  • WATER. Hydrogen gas is given off which ignites. Corrosive liquid spits out. DANGER

  • Preparing sodium for use: All equipment must be dry.
  • Use forceps to TT place a piece of sodium onto a tile.
  • Use a sharp knife to cut off the Sodium DANGER (Y12) required piece.
  • Immediately return the unwanted sodium to its bottle (See reverse) and close lid.
  • Dab the cut piece of sodium with a paper tissue or filter paper to remove the oil (medicinal paraffin).
  • Do not use organic solvents, such as petroleum spirit or hexane to remove the oil; this has caused fires when clearing up.

Waste / disposal

  • : WSpec, see note below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE for small amounts only: 3
  • WSpec: Add the metal (no more than 5 g) in small pieces (~ 4 mm sides) a few at a time, to 100 cm of propan-2-ol (iso-propanol) in a glass beaker. Place a heat-proof mat over the beaker to extinguish any Disposal flames caused by the propan-2-ol vapour catching fire. Allow the reaction to subside before adding more pieces of sodium. Once reaction is complete, carefully add solution to a bucket of cold water and pour down a foul- water drain.

107Zinc
CLEAPSS HazCard PDF first page: 347

Compounds / substances

Zinc powder

Zn MW 65.38
Direct compound anchor: #compound-107-1-zinc-powder

Zinc granules, foil

Zn MW 65.38
Direct compound anchor: #compound-107-2-zinc-granules-foil

Warnings

107 Risk Assessment Guidance Zinc Zinc powder Zn (65.38) In contact with water releases flammable gases which ignite spontaneously [H260].
Catches fire spontaneously if exposed to air [H250].
Very toxic to aquatic life with long lasting effects [H410].
Currently not classified as hazardous.

Do not mix / dangerous contact

  • WATER. ALKALINE SOLUTIONS. Moist zinc dust can ignite spontaneously.
  • ACIDS and ALKALIS. Hydrogen gas (extremely flammable) is formed.
  • IODINE, MANGANESE(IV) OXIDE, POTASSIUM CHLORATE(V). Violent reactions can occur.
  • SULFUR. A violent or explosive reaction occurs.
  • AMMONIUM SULFIDE SOLUTION. Hydrogen sulfide gas (extremely flammable, very toxic) is evolved. DANGER Zinc residues (powder/dust) from some practical activities have ignited, leading to fires (eg, in waste bins), when incorrectly disposed of. Follow correct disposal procedures.

Important notes

  • General use: Reactions with dilute acids/alkalis and salts may be carried Zinc out on a test-tube scale using zinc granules or powder. powder/dust DANGER
  • Burning zinc powder (TT): The zinc oxide fumes produced (also called (See reverse) ‘philosopher’s wool’) when the powder burns are considered hazardous. Y7 Ensure the laboratory is well-ventilated. Do not inhale fumes.
  • Large-scale preparation of hydrogen (TT): See Recipe 44. Zinc Currently not classified foil/granules as hazardous

Waste / disposal

  • : see notes below Follow general guidance in About Hazcards (GL 120). NOTE for small amounts:
  • W5 (zinc powder/dust). Disposal
  • W3 or W8 (zinc granules/foil). Note: Zinc residues (powder/dust) from some practical activities have ignited, leading to fires (eg, in waste bins) when incorrectly disposed of. Check activity-specific guidance.
